The Manichaean Country & the Buddhist Invasion of Indochina

The Manichaean Country & the Buddhist Invasion of Indochina

A History of Mankind (424)

David Roman

Nov 22

Preview

READ IN APP

Just as Tibet became a Buddhist stronghold, Central Asian Uyghurs grabbed a chunk or the northern Tibetan plateau, which they controlled for some time. There they established one of the strangest states in history.

Being traditional Chinese adversaries who had often allied with Tibetan rulers, Uyghur Turks combined Buddhism with their own shamanistic beliefs and heavy elements of Manicheism imported from Sogdiana and China¹, to the point that the Uyghur Khaganate that survived to the mid-9th century has sometimes being called the world’s only Manichaean state – possibly an exaggeration, but not an outright falsehood².

Manichaeism had made some inroads into Tibet and China in particular since the An Lushan Rebellion³, together with other religions definitely seen as foreign and alien by Chinese officialdom, like Zoroastrianism and Christianity. They were all affected by the anti-Buddhist persecution launched by Emperor Wuzong and, in effect, stamped out of the country; the persecution, indeed, spilled into Uyghur lands on the back of advancing Chinese armies, and resulted in the destruction of many Manichaean temples and the murder of many priests.

By the time Xuanzong II rose to the Chinese throne, the Uyghur Khaganate was all but dead, crushed by the combined pressure of Chinese from the east and Kyrgyz Turks from the north, many of whom migrated south from today’s Tuva region in Russia. Manicheism was slowly driven underground, never to reappear as an organized religion with large masses of adherents and public festivals, although Buddhism didn’t really occupy a vacuum that soon was filled by Islam instead.

The Uyghur Khaganate at its maximum extension.

Siddharta’s old religion displayed more energy in Afghanistan, where the region around Kabul remained a Buddhist stronghold well after the rise of Islam, under the rule of the Shahi Turks. It was during this era when the last stages of development of the Bamiyan Buddhas complex was conducted, right before the region came under the influence of Muslim Iranians⁴; the Shahi themselves demonstrated remarkable religious flexibility during this transition: they first became Muslim and then Hindu under Indian influence, when Islam again lost traction in Afghanistan, before they were finally expelled by Muslim Turks between the 10th and 11th centuries.

Across Southeast Asia, Buddhism was quickly embraced by elites keen to demonstrate their ties with the more civilized, richer states. In the 9th century, flush with cash from foreign raiding and trade, Java’s Sailendra Dynasty ruling the Viking-like Malay state of Srivijaya found the skilled labor to build temples like Borobudur/Barabudur⁵, the world’s largest Buddhist temple, and one that smacks of nouveau riche striving, as if the Sailendra were anxious to keep up with rulers of more established states that they regularly attacked...

Embodied Harness: What Does It Take to Build a Robot Agent?

Embodied AI beyond VLAs and WAMs. Let's explore how to take harness in real world and what it can bring for the future of agentic robotics ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

November 21, 2026 | Read online

AI 101: Embodied Harness: What Does It Take to Build a Robot Agent?

Embodied AI beyond VLAs and WAMs. Let's explore how to take harness in real world and what it can bring for the future of agentic robotics

Alyona Vert.

For the past few months, we’ve been exploring agentic harnesses – it has been an especially popular topic on our YouTube channel, and we can clearly say people want even more of it. A harness is the software scaffolding wrapped around an AI model that gives it memory, tools, and guardrails to get things done. Though some argued that frontier models starting to "eat" simpler harness logic, the "model + harness" combo has proven itself as a system that really works. There are at least three reasons why:

Heavy harness infrastructure is still essential for long-horizon tasks that agents have to work on continuously for days.

Harness can be built out of modular, plug-and-play components, and there is huge potential in the idea of a harness compiling custom harnesses on demand.

And the real magic is that the exact same model can act like a completely different assistant depending on the harness it is running in.

So far, we've mostly talked about harnesses for digital agents. Today, we have numerous autonomous coding agents that can genuinely tackle long-running tasks and build software on their own. That was confirmed both at the AIE Code Summit and in our recent interview with Simon Willison. But through all of this, the topic of harnesses in robotics has barely been touched.

What if we adapt this setup to the physical world, in other words, make harness embodied and pair it with foundation models that understand 3D space, video, and physical actions?

Recently, Grid Robotics took on this challenge, laying out a blueprint for how to build agentic robotics capable of handling the same level of complexity as today's coding agents (or even higher), but in the 3D world.

Their “Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems“ research caught our eye because it takes a broad look at both robotics and harness design. So let's explore it together and see what this roadmap offers on the path toward embodied agents we can finally trust to operate on their own.

Let’s go!

In today’s episode:

Why robotics needs a broader approach than VLAs and WAMs

What an embodied harness does and how it differs from a digital harness

The full workflow: What happens before and after each model call

The world doesn’t pause while the model thinks, and what happens when the user changes their mind

How to test the robot, not just the model

What makes it an embodied agent

Why do we need a broader paradigm for robotics beyond VLAs and WAMs?

Firstly, let’s turn back to the definitions of VLA and WAM that we discussed in our previous episodes.

VLAs, Vision-Language-Action models, turn what a robot sees into an action. They take camera observations, a language instruction, and sometimes the robot’s state, then predict actions.

WAMs, World Action Models, predict how the physical world may change and connect these predictions with action generation. Depending on its design, they might predict future observations alongside actions or use predicted futures to help generate them.

These model types both describe what a model does, but generalization appears to be a weak spot. In real world, either the scene rapidly changes or an instruction can be phrased differently, so performance often drops if models can't keep up with changing conditions.

But yet, VLAs and WAMs as foundation models are a promising starting point for robots that can handle a broader range of tasks. And this range is a matter of responsibility. Grid Robotics pursue creating robots which have the longer-term goal as sustained responsibility – only this way they can reliably manage workflows and recurring roles. That is a criterion of agentic robotics.

That’s why Grid Robotics’ researchers proposed a roadmap for Agentic Robotics. No, they don’t want to replace VLAs or WAMs. It is more of a gradual expansion in scope: How do we coordinate multiple actions so a robot can complete a task and verify the result?

Their idea is that a foundation model becomes the decision-making core of a robot agent when it works together with an Embodied Harness: the surrounding software that gives the model context, connects reasoning to observations, skills and tools, execution, and verification, and manages what happens when those tools act in the physical world. These two parts form a working loop of an embodied agent.

From Policy-Centric Robotics toward Agentic Robotics

Today, we mostly see policy-centric systems. In this setup, an action policy takes a task instruction and sensor readings, then generates action targets for the robot to execute – often incorporating reasoning, subtask prediction, or real-time adjustments.

In an agentic system, an agent is the decision center that decides how to pursue the goal: it can call skills and tools, check what happened, and adjust its next decision. The Grid Robotics’ researchers describe this system with three functional roles:

Layer

Role

Examples & Functions

System 2 (S2)

High-level decision center

Interprets the user's goal, considers constraints, decides how to proceed, selects skills and tools, and checks outcomes.

System 1 (S1)

Callable skills and tools

VLA or WAM policies, object detection, motion planning, and navigation skills.

System 0 (S0)

Real-time body & local control

Joint movements, balance, trajectory tracking, and continuous real-time operations.

Here, the most interested for us is S2 which combines a foundation model with an embodied harness. The idea reminds how these elements work in digital AI agent. The foundation model provides knowledge and reasoning, and the embodied harness supplies everything around: determines what information reaches the model, what it can act on, how the system follows through on its decisions, and maintains plans and memory.

We will return to how the entire agentic robot loop works later. First, we need to make clear the embodied harness’ functions →

What does the Embodied Harness do?

There are five core components that form all embodied harness jobs:

(BTW, even if you don’t work in robotics, it’s super interesting to see how an agent harness works in the physical world.)

What happens when a rescue robot gets new instructions halfway through a search? In the full guide, we break down the five parts of an embodied harness and follow a robot through a collapsed mine. See how it checks what happened after each move and changes course when the team redirects it. We’ll also look at how to test the system while the world keeps moving.

The president has nominated a lackey to look into lawbreaking that his own office committed.

Trump Scrambles to Put Arsonist in Charge of Fire Investigations

The president has nominated a lackey to look into lawbreaking that his own office committed.

Catherine Rampell • FREE PREVIEW

READ TO ME

Hey everyone, and welcome to this week’s Receipts.

Pop quiz: Who should decide whether the Trump administration broke the law when it refused to spend money Congress ordered it to spend? If you answered “the guy who helped do it,” congratulations: Senate Republicans appear to agree with you.

This week, Trump’s (very partisan) nominee to lead the (very nonpartisan) Government Accountability Office, Eric Ueland, got a fast-tracked confirmation hearing, and it may be one of the most consequential things the lame-duck Senate does. Today’s newsletter addresses why a boring-sounding auditing job at an agency most Americans have never heard of is worth your attention . . . and why my recent newsletter cheerily outlining Democratic lawmakers’ plans to reclaim their constitutional power of the purse may have been, er, premature.

If stories like this—focused on accountability for the Trump administration—matter to you, I hope you’ll consider becoming a Bulwark+ member. In 2027, accountability is going to be an even bigger focus of our journalism, and it’s our members who make all our work possible. You can join right here:

Support Our Accountability Coverage

Join Bulwark+ today

Meanwhile, thanks for reading—and a happy Thanksgiving to you and yours.

–Catherine

(Photo illustration by Sarah Rogers/The Bulwark | Photos: Getty, Shutterstock)

IF DONALD TRUMP HAS HIS WAY, investigations into whether he broke budget law will soon be led by a guy who probably helped him do it.

Right now there’s an opening for the top job at the Government Accountability Office, a century-old, independent, nonpartisan agency that serves as Congress’s investigative and auditing arm—sort of like an inspector general for the entire federal government. GAO and its staff of 3,200 are tasked with examining how well tax dollars are spent (or, in the case at issue now, not spent) and with rooting out waste, fraud, and abuse throughout the federal government. As its name suggests, GAO is the primary tool lawmakers have to hold the executive branch accountable when it disobeys Congress.¹ It is also the only entity with clear authority to sue the president on Congress’s behalf.

Of course, to Trump, these are bugs, not features. Which is why he has asked the Republican Senate majority to do him a solid on its way out the door: shield him from accountability, and constitutionally castrate the entire legislative branch, by confirming a Trump lackey, Eric Ueland, to run the GAO.

I realize that fretting about this kind of good-government stuff may sound quaint, particularly as our president literally bear-hugs dictators. (Please, will no one think of the auditors???) So let me explain why this confirmation would be a serious threat to our constitutional separation of powers—and a ticket for more corruption and white-collar criming throughout not just the executive branch but parts of the private sector, too...

The Light Is Better Than the Darkness

What a politician, a priest, and a gang member taught me about love and politics.

Jon Favreau and Crooked Media

Nov 21

Preview

READ IN APP

I first met James Talarico almost exactly a year ago, when he first started running for Senate. He talked about people wanting Democrats to fight and people wanting a politics of love, so I asked him to reconcile those two things – to explain what fighting with love actually looks like.

He told me about his mother, and the night she escaped his abusive father when James was a baby. She packed their belongings into her car, found a room at the hotel where she worked, and eventually moved into a small apartment in East Austin. There wasn’t room for a nursery, so she put his crib in a closet and decorated it with pictures and toys.

A few weeks ago, on the night he became the first Democrat in nearly forty years to win a Senate race in Texas, James went back to that story:

Love can turn an enemy into a friend.

He said it from a podium with a sign that read “Love Thy Neighbor.” Online, and even in some of my group chats, the reaction was the verbal equivalent of an eyeroll:

“Cheesy.”

“Cringey.”

“Not for me.”

I’ll admit that even I wondered if the sign was a bit much.

But Talarico had been dealing with this kind of cynicism the entire campaign. At one event in Laredo, someone asked him, “You preach about love, but isn’t that just too weak for the moment that we’re in today?”

I get it. The last decade of American politics has given us countless reasons to doubt that love is a winning strategy. Not a lot of love in the headlines or on our screens or in our feeds. Plenty of cruelty, though. Plenty of fear and anger and pain. Constant reminders that life is often unfair and unjust and terribly random in the misfortune it deals us.

My friend Father Greg Boyle has a way of describing our response to all this:...

Week signals: Nadir of the summit

Plus: watch points for India, Nepal, the Balkans, the US, Australia, and Taiwan.

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

Week signals: Nadir of the summit

Plus: watch points for India, Nepal, the Balkans, the US, Australia, and Taiwan.

Michael Feller

Nov 21

Preview

READ IN APP

Map of the Sudetenland accompanying the Godesberg Memorandum (September 24, 1938), Czech Foreign Ministry, Prague.

Hello,

In this edition of Week Signals:

IN REVIEW. Less Yalta, more Munich; grand theft politics; Shenzhen and the art of alliance maintenance.

UP AHEAD. Summits in Delhi and Brussels; volatility on Wall Street; elections in Australia and Taiwan.

Upgrade to paid

The Week in Review: Piecemeal in our time

The week began with grounded flights and software issues from banks to iPhones. It ended, unsurprisingly, with a Nasdaq crash that even Claude couldn’t fix. It was all bad timing for Donald Trump’s visit to the high-tech metropolis of Shenzhen. Yet judging from his reaction to the allegations that Nvidia’s former CEO had overseen a massive chip smuggling operation, maybe Trump doesn’t really care about the US’s competitive edge.

And perhaps he doesn’t care about the US’s waning alliance-based hegemony either, judging by the spectacle of his trilateral meeting with Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin following the short shrift he gave the Quad earlier in the week. And while it’s always smart to engage with your adversaries, this ostensible Yalta-style summit – a “Big Three” for the 21st century – seemed more a way to put Putin on a pedestal while kowtowing to Xi. Luckily, the summit seems to have been without much consequence, other than a “declaration of peace” (ending which wars? you may ask). Indeed, if there were to be any real outcome, they might not be very happy for the likes of Ukraine, Taiwan, or Canada.

But a Yalta this was not. Not only was there no evidence of a 1945-style global carve-up, but Trump, Putin and Xi are not the victors of a major war, unlike Roosevelt, Stalin and Churchill before them. Indeed, two of the three are still fighting conflicts they started, and aren’t doing especially well either, despite their nuclear weapons and comparative economic heft. And regardless of what one might think of Stalin, Churchill or FDR, none of the current three has anything approaching the world historical stature (at least not yet). For Shenzhen, appropriately enough, this was a Temu Yalta. And without the money-back guarantee if things fall apart.

So maybe the Yalta comparison, already dodgy and self-serving (at least to Putin, whose soldiers now reoccupy the Crimean resort), is the wrong one. Returning to World War II and any comparisons of where we are now in our own geopolitical cycle, perhaps the better comparison is Munich, 1938, where a hapless Western leader travelled to meet Hitler and Mussolini to head off a global conflict over a quarrel in a faraway Eastern European country “between people of whom we know nothing”.

Yet Neville Chamberlain, who said those words in the days before his infamous deal on the Sudetenland, was no Hitler-apolgist patsy. Serious, war-weary, and realist, he held the Führer in deserved contempt. And while, like his brother Austen Chamberlain, he had a bit more time for Mussolini, his subsequent record in Churchill’s war cabinet should show that he was not the appeaser he’s commonly remembered as.

As for Trump on Putin and the Donbas? I’ll let you be the judge, but the omens for “peace in our time” are probably just as portentous as they were in 1938. And while this doesn’t mean world war, it doesn’t mean its absence either. Indeed, if you look at the state of cybersecurity – from exploding refrigerators in Europe to the now daily system compromises in the US – the war has already begunAnd this brings us back to Shenzhen and one of Trump’s fellow travellers, Howard Lutnick, of whom we wrote last week. Lutnick, it must be said, has longstanding links with Russia, just like his boss. A 2021 FBI report unearthed in the Epstein files release – corroborating allegations elsewhere that Lutnick’s Cantor Fitzgerald was the go-to bank for New York’s Russian mafia – may not stand up in court, but Cantor’s links to Russia are well established (as are its links to China, Nvidia and the rest of the semiconductor complex). All this may be circumstantial for a successful global businessman who has taken a financial sacrifice in order to serve in public office, but we weren’t born yesterday.

As with the Epstein files, we can’t expect much clarity from the Department of Justice on what the links may or may not be between the US, Russian and Chinese administrations. And even if there were to be an investigation, as under Biden or during Trump’s first term, it would all soon get sullied as a witch hunt. All we can go on, in divining the motivating factors behind Shenzhen, the Ukraine-Russia peace negotiations, or the war in Iran, is to take at face value what our leaders are telling us.

Yet as in Munich 1938, all leaders lie and some leaders lie all the time. What might be called a declaration of peace, or a policy of realist appeasement, could break apart as quickly as any of the equally flimsy liberal internationalist norms that shattered in the 1930s and are in the process of shattering today.

And of these norms, perhaps the most important is the concept of alliances. Unlike the First World War, where it was arguably rigid alliances that led to war in Europe (Germany entered because it was allied to Austria-Hungary; France entered because it was allied to Russia, which was allied to Serbia), the Second World War came about because of doubts on alliances. After Czechoslovakia was shorn of its German-speaking territories, Hitler tried his luck on Poland. This, of course, was a miscalculation, but it was a miscalculation that was shared.

The long and short of it is that we are in times ripe for miscalculation too, and not just of the financial kind (witness those evaporating tech valuations). One thing that Trump, Putin and Xi have in common is that they’ve sacked many of their most senior military commanders, or – in the case of the latter two – have had them killed. Another thing is that they don’t seem to invite external counsel (or even the internal kind, judging from how Marco Rubio’s alleged report on Nvidia was received). Trusted, dispassionate and expert advice is essential to strategy, as any successful leader knows. These three, like the Big Three before them, may consider themselves a success. But only history can decide. ...

Vessel erupts in flames after strike in Gulf of Finland; Nato vows to defend ‘every inch’ of Polish territory. What we know on day 1,733 A Russian cargo ship was hit by a Ukrainian drone and caught fire in the Baltic Sea on Saturday, as Finland’s prime minister said Kyiv had the right to hit back against its “aggressor”. The Finnish prime minister, Petteri Orpo, suggested the incident in the Gulf of Finland had to do with Russia’s war in Ukraine. “War is spilling over into our immediate vicinity,” Orpo said on X. “Ukraine has the right to fight back against the aggressor.” Hours later, a Russian helicopter was suspected of violating Finnish airspace , according to the Finnish defence minister, who drew a possible link between the two incidents. “The suspected airspace violation may be linked to rescue or monitoring operations involving a cargo ship that was damaged in the Gulf of Finland,” Antti Hakkanen said on X. Petteri Blomvall, an officer at the Finnish Border Guard, said: “I can confirm that a Ukrainian drone strike hit the ship in the morning that caused the fire … The crew from the ship has been evacuated, the ship is anchored and the firefighting is ongoing.” The Finnish Defence Forces identified the ship as the Sparta II, which is under Ukrainian and US sanctions , according to the site Opensanctions. The website said the ship was suspected of transporting weapons from Syria to Russia. The Marine Traffic maritime monitoring website said the Sparta II had departed St Petersburg around 11pm and was bound for the port of Baltiysk, in the Russian exclave of Kaliningrad. Finland’s defence ministry said it had immediately informed Nato of the drone strike It also said temporary restrictions were being enforced in the airspace and maritime area of the eastern part of the Gulf. Rattled by Russia’s invasion, Finland joined Nato in 2023, dropping decades of military non-alignment. The Gulf of Finland is a key maritime passage for Russia, leading to the former capital St Petersburg. “I can guarantee that Nato will defend every inch of Polish territory,” Nato’s secretary general, Mark Rutte, said in Warsaw on Saturday, after two Russian missiles and a drone crashed in its territory on Friday. Poland accused the Kremlin of “intentional provocation” after the incident, as it occurred near where thousands of European troops allied with Ukraine were holding training exercises. A meeting of Nato’s North Atlantic Council will be held in Brussels on Monday to discuss the violation of Polish airspace by Russian missiles. “In the near future, we will receive strong support from our NATO allies to bolster the protection of our eastern border,” Polish defence minister Wladyslaw Kosiniak-Kamysz wrote on X on Saturday. Korean consortium wins Vietnam high-speed rail design contract. The Korea National Railway and two Korean engineering firms will take part in a project to develop a high-speed railway connecting northern and southern Vietnam, spanning 1,541 kilometers between Hanoi and Ho Chi Minh City, according to Korea's Ministry of Land, Infrastructure and Transport, Sunday. A consortium comprising Korean, French and Vietnamese companies has won a contract for a feasibility study and front-end engineering design (FEED) for the project. The contract was tendered by the Thang Long Project Management Unit under Vietnam's Ministry of Construction. The consortium includes state-run Korea National Railway and Korean engineering firms Yooshin and Sambo, along with France's Systra and Vietnam's Tedi, both of which specialize in railway design. The consortium won the contract in competition with 40 other bidders, according to the ministry. The initial phase of the project is valued at 560 billion won ($416 million), with the consortium set to carry out the work over 30 months. Jim Handy, founder and general director at Objective Analysis, said in an interview with The Korea Times that the surge in memory prices — caused by a sharp increase in demand — is “temporary,” echoing concerns among some Korean analysts that the current memory cycle may be nearing its peak. “I do not think that AI demand is growing that quickly, so they are likely to slow their spending at some point, and that will cause memory prices to collapse,” Handy said. Although the timing of a downturn is difficult to predict, memory prices could eventually fall back to 2024 levels once demand weakens, he said. Handy has raised particular concerns over the scale of spending by hyperscalers, such as Google, Meta, Amazon and Open AI. Korea's car trade deficit with China hits record on Tesla EV imports. South Korea's deficit in the car trade with China rose to a record high in the January-October period on rising demand for Tesla electric vehicles (EVs) made in China, industry data showed Sunday. South Korea exported $168.77 million worth of cars to China in the first 10 months of the year, down 34.9 percent from the same period last year, according to data from the Korea International Trade Association (KITA) and the Ministry of Trade, Industry and Resources. However, its imports of cars from China surged 79.2 percent on-year to $4.39 billion over the cited period, bringing the trade deficit to a record $4.23 billion. South Korea has recorded a deficit in the car trade with China every year since 2017, with the gap widening sharply from $466 million in 2022 to $943 million in 2023 and $1.73 billion in 2024. The widening deficit is largely attributed to strong sales of Tesla EVs manufactured at the U.S. automaker's Gigafactory in Shanghai. Samsung Electronics, SK hynix dividend payout ratios surge through Q3 amid AI boom. Samsung Electronics and SK hynix saw their dividend payout ratios rise sharply through the third quarter under their shareholder return policies, data showed Sunday, amid the memory chip boom driven by artificial intelligence (AI). According to the companies' respective quarterly reports, Samsung Electronics' dividend payout ratio stood at 18 percent on a consolidated basis as of end-September, up from 4.1 percent at the end of the first half, while SK hynix's ratio rose to 8.4 percent from 0.4 percent over the same period. A dividend payout ratio refers to the proportion of a company's earnings distributed to shareholders as dividends, serving as an indicator of how a company allocates its profits. The sharp increases came as the two chipmakers announced plans to distribute sizable dividends to shareholders as part of their respective shareholder return policies. Samsung Electronics decided to pay 30 trillion won ($22.3 billion) in dividends for the third quarter, or 4,601 won per share, compared with 372 won per common share in the first quarter and 374 won in the second quarter. Apple Intelligence, decoded: How to make sense of Apple’s confusing AI lineup

Apple Intelligence, decoded: How to make sense of Apple’s confusing AI lineup. Apple has received much criticism for being late to the AI game. While Open AI helped define the AI boom beginning in late 2021, and Google and other tech giants quickly jumped on the AI train the following year, Apple didn’t make its first real foray into artificial intelligence until 2024 with Apple Intelligence. Though the initial capabilities of Apple Intelligence were rather lackluster compared with its peers, Apple spent the next two years rapidly refining its offerings. In 2026, Apple made its AI system much more capable and expansive by introducing more advanced AI tools, including the Siri AI chatbot and Audio Intelligence And just last month, Apple also debuted an additional AI tool dubbed “intelligent assistant.” The thing is, Apple now has so many AI offerings that it’s getting hard for the average consumer to keep track of what each one does, which devices support which AI tool, and which tools require a fee to access. So, here’s a cheat sheet for making sense of Apple’s increasingly confusing AI lineup. Apple Intelligence Apple Intelligence is the oldest of the company’s modern AI offerings, introduced in 2024. Think of Apple Intelligence as the foundational artificial intelligence layer integrated into Apple’s operating systems, including i OS, mac OS, and watch OS. Apple Intelligence includes a suite of smaller AI tools tailored to specific tasks. For example, Apple Intelligence powers Visual Intelligence, which lets Apple’s AI see and understand what’s on your screen or through your camera and take action on it; for example, it can tell you the nutritional information of the food on your plate. Apple Intelligence powers Audio Intelligence , a tool that can help you identify sounds and music and even recap your spoken conversations on some Apple Watches. It is also the engine behind numerous other AI features across your Apple products, including image generation, live translation, select photo editing tools, message summaries, and more. Not every device that supports Apple Intelligence supports all its features. Any Mac with an Apple Silicon chip will run Apple Intelligence, but you’ll need a Mac with an M3 chip and 12 GB of RAM or more to use some of its most advanced features, including custom Siri Voices and advanced AI dictation. For the i Phone, you’ll need an i Phone 17 Pro or later to use those advanced Apple Intelligence features (i Phone 15 Pro and later is required for most Apple Intelligence features). As for Audio Intelligence on the Apple Watch, that’s limited to the new Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Series 4. Siri AI Siri AI is Apple’s new chatbot. Though people often use the terms “Siri AI” and “Apple Intelligence” interchangeably, they are actually distinct. Apple Intelligence is the foundational AI system integrated into Apple’s operating systems. Siri AI is the natural-language gateway to Apple Intelligence—it lets.

US DOJ Charges Suspected Russian Agent for Stalking Palantir Employees in California

US DOJ Charges Suspected Russian Agent for Stalking Palantir Employees in California. "We will not tolerate any efforts — by foreign governments or otherwise — to turn Silicon Valley into a base for foreign intelligence operations," said U.S. Attorney Craig H. Missakian. The alleged Russian agent was arrested in October for "stalking and harassing employees of the software and AI firm Palantir, sources told ABC News, because of its assistance to Ukraine in its war with Russia." The indictment says D'iu traveled from Istanbul to Mexico City last June and was connected with a person who helped him illegally cross the southern U.S. border. Once in the U.S., he was instructed to purchase laptops, cellphones and remote access software that his co-conspirators used to help him in his alleged targeting of the company's employees. At one point, prosecutors say D'iu was directed by his co-conspirators to visit a coffee shop where many of the company's employees would work and try to identify them and take pictures of their screens while they were working. In September, prosecutors say D'iu allegedly followed a female employee of the company in his vehicle for nearly 30 miles after she left the company's Palo Alto, California, office. Even as the employee attempted various maneuvers to evade him, D'iu aggressively sought to follow her until she ultimately phoned police and was instructed to drive to the San Francisco Police Department's Mission Station. The nonprofit news site Palo Alto Online notes the government's affidavit "identified at least two other instances in early September in which employees of the defense company reported being followed by a car matching the description of D'iu's silver Lexus SUV. The coffee shops and other locations where D'iu stationed his laptop — sometimes for hours at a time — are in close proximity to the Palantir office in downtown Palo Alto. FBI agents executed a search warrant on Oct. 8 at D'iu's residence in East Palo Alto. There they found the California state ID, passport and Social Security card that were present in the DHL packages, as well as two Russian passports, $18,000 in cash and a notebook with 10 Palo Alto area addresses. Five of them correlate to the home addresses of employees at the unnamed Silicon Valley defense company. The agents also recovered three employee "target package profiles" that included their names, names of relatives, education and employment history, leadership qualities and a list of strengths and weaknesses. After searching D'iu's phone, the FBI found messages from a Telegram contact with photos of four employees and their addresses. D'iu was instructed to act like a food delivery driver and knock on their doors to verify if the employees did live at those addresses, according to the affidavit. If convicted of all charges, D'iu.

Toyota, Nissan, and Honda to Standardize Parts to Lower Costs

Toyota, Nissan, and Honda to Standardize Parts to Lower Costs. Car and Driver reports: Japan's three largest automakers have agreed to begin standardizing vehicle parts in an effort to reduce costs, according to Automotive News. In the short term, the standardization efforts will begin with unified quality criteria for steel, consistent protocols for plastics and resins, and a universal color-coding scheme for wiring harnesses. In the long term, the Japan Automobile Manufacturers Association has plans to move to more components that cover the entire car. The push to simplify and lower costs comes as Japan's seven automakers, including Toyota, Nissan, Honda, Mazda, Subaru, Mitsubishi, and Suzuki, work to fend off the increasingly competitive list of Chinese automakers. Long-time Slashdot reader sinij asks a related question. "Can we get a standard infotainment port/spec so we can have third-party market for this? Will AI-Fueled Cyberattacks Lead to Better Funding for Critical Open Source Projects?

Will AI-Fueled Cyberattacks Lead to Better Funding for Critical Open Source Projects?. Remember that famous XKCD comic joking that all modern digital infrastructure rests on "a project some random person in Nebraska has been thanklessly maintaining since 2003." Politico Europe's cybersecurity reporter wonders if this recent spate of outages suggests that supporting block is coming loose? (Especially after Politico reported Thursday that internal services at EU institutions went offline after a major AI-fueled cyberattack.) Authorities blame the sudden wave of instability on the capacity of new AI tools to detect vulnerabilities in existing code and for bad actors to exploit them. Interviews with more than a dozen cybersecurity leaders and those working on maintaining the internet show the scale of the challenge to keep the web up and running, in a world where the pace of finding vulnerabilities has rapidly and permanently accelerated. Since the web's early days, an unruly cast of idealist engineers and non-profit foundations have been essential for looking after decades-old critical code that's been used to build our digital world. This small army of largely volunteer coders now say they can't keep up with the power and pace of AI. "We're running on fumes," said Robin Bender Ginn, executive director of the Open JS Foundation, which looks after Java Script, among the most widely used programming languages. "It keeps me up at night." Cybersecurity agencies around the world rang the alarm last month about major flaws detected in PHP, another critical coding language that underpins 70% of the web. This week, similar scares have spread over Java and NGINX, which also fuel large swathes of technology. Helmut Reisinger, the European chief executive of cybersecurity giant Palo Alto Networks, said in an interview that the company is seeing "more or less" ten times as many incidents compared to about four months ago. He predicts the constant drumbeat of attacks and scares in the last month is going to become the "new norm. " The rise of AI models and their capabilities of finding software flaws is putting more pressure on open-source software engineers, who are inundated with reports of vulnerabilities — some of which are false or exaggerated. Reports of flaws discovered by AI are "quite verbose," Ginn said. Juhan Lepassaar, the head of EU cyber agency ENISA, said in an interview that the agency has observed a similar pattern of false positives. While some hope the AI-fueled attacks "will put the needs of open source software on the map and prompt an intervention," according to the article, "many of those steeped in the ecosystem. have their doubts." "I'm a bit pessimistic," says Nils Adermann, a PHP Foundation board member. "I worry that people will just not care enough to actually do anything about it." of this story at Slashdot. .
